Infinitary cut-elimination via finite approximations (extended version)
Matteo Acclavio, Gianluca Curzi, Giulio Guerrieri

Abstract
We investigate non-wellfounded proof systems based on parsimonious logic, a weaker variant of linear logic where the exponential modality ! is interpreted as a constructor for streams over finite data. Logical consistency is maintained at a global level by adapting a standard progressing criterion. We present an infinitary version of cut-elimination based on finite approximations, and we prove that, in presence of the progressing criterion, it returns well-defined non-wellfounded proofs at its limit. Furthermore, we show that cut-elimination preserves the progressive criterion and various regularity conditions internalizing degrees of proof-theoretical uniformity. Finally, we provide a denotational semantics for our systems based on the relational model.

1
Introduction

Non-wellfounded proof theory
studies proofs as possibly infinite (but finitely branching) trees, where logical consistency is maintained via global conditions called
progressing
(or
validity
)
criteria
. In this setting, the so-called
regular
(also called
circular
) proofs receive a special attention, as they admit a finite description in terms of (possibly cyclic) directed graphs.

This area of proof theory makes its first appearance (in its modern guise) in the modal
\mu
-calculus
[
28
,
13
]
. Since then, it has been extensively investigated from many perspectives (see, e.g.,
[
7
,
33
,
12
,
21
]
), establishing itself as an ideal setting for manipulating least and greatest fixed points, and hence for modeling induction and coinduction principles.

Non-wellfounded proof theory has been applied to constructive fixed point logics i.e., with a computational interpretation based on the
Curry-Howard correspondence

[
34
]
. A key example can be found in the context of
linear logic
(
\mathsf{LL}
)
[
19
]
, a logic implementing a finer control on resources thanks to the
exponential
modalities
\oc
and
\wn
. In this framework, the most extensively studied fixed point logic is
\mu\mathsf{MALL}
, defined as the exponential-free fragment of
\mathsf{LL}
with least and greatest fixed point operators (respectively,
\mu
and its dual
\nu
)
[
6
,
5
]
.

In
[
6
]
Baelde and Miller have shown that the exponentials can be recovered in
\mu\mathsf{MALL}
by exploiting the fixed points operators, i.e., by defining
\oc A\coloneqq\nu X.(\mathbf{1}\with A\with(X\otimes X))
and
\wn A\coloneqq\mu X.(\bot\oplus A\oplus(X\invamp X))
.
As these authors notice, the fixed point-based definition of
\oc
and
\wn
can be regarded as a more permissive variant of the standard exponentials, since a proof of
\nu X.(\mathbf{1}\with A\with(X\otimes X))
could be constructed using different proofs of
A
, whereas in
\mathsf{LL}
a proof of
\oc A
is constructed uniformly using a single proof of
A
.
This proof-theoretical notion of
non-uniformity
is indeed a central feature of the fixed-point exponentials.

However, the above encoding is not free of issues. First, as discussed in full detail in
[
16
]
, the encoding of the exponentials does not verify the Seely isomorphisms, syntactically expressed by the equivalence
\oc(A\with B)\multimapboth(\oc A\otimes\oc B)
, an essential property for modeling exponentials in
\mathsf{LL}
. Specifically, the fixed-point definition of
\oc
relies on the multiplicative connective
\otimes
, which forces an interpretation of
\oc A
based on lists rather than multisets. Secondly, as pointed out in
[
6
]
, there is a neat mismatch between cut-elimination for the exponentials of
\mathsf{LL}

and the one for the fixed point exponentials of
\mu\mathsf{MALL}
. While the first problem is related to syntactic deficiencies of the encoding, and does not undermine further investigations on fixed point-based definitions of the exponential modalities, the second one is more critical. These apparent differences between the two exponentials contribute to stressing an important aspect in linear logic modalities, i.e., their
non-canonicity

[
30
,
11
]
1
1

1

One can construct
\mathsf{LL}
proof systems with alternative (not equivalent) exponential modalities, see
[
27
]
.

.

On a parallel research thread, Mazza
[
24
,
25
,
26
]
studied
parsimonious logic
, a variant of linear logic where the exponential modality
\oc
satisfies Milner’s law (i.e.,
\oc A\multimapboth A\otimes\oc A
) and invalidates the implications
\oc A\multimap\oc\oc A
(
digging
) and
\oc A\multimap\oc A\otimes\oc A
(
contraction
).
In parsimonious logic, a proof of
\oc A
can be interpreted as a
stream
over (a finite set of) proofs of
A
, i.e., as a greatest fixed point, where the linear implications
A\otimes\oc A\multimap\oc A
(
co-absorption
) and
\oc A\multimap A\otimes\oc A
(
absorption
) can be read computationally as
the
push
and
pop

operations on streams. More specifically, a formula
\oc A
is introduced by an
infinitely branching rule
that takes a finite set of proofs
\mathcal{D}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{D}_{n}
of
A
and a (possibly non-recursive) function
f:\mathbb{N}\to\{1,\ldots,n\}
as premises, and constructs a proof of
\oc A
representing a stream of proofs of the form
\mathfrak{S}=({\mathcal{D}_{f(0)}},{\mathcal{D}_{f(1)}},\ldots,\allowbreak{\mathcal{D}_{f(n)}},\ldots)
. Hence, parsimonious logic exponential modalities exploit in an essential way the above-mentioned proof-theoretical non-uniformity, which in turn deeply interfaces with notions of non-uniformity from computational complexity
[
26
]
.

The analysis of parsimonious logic conducted in
[
25
,
26
]
reveals that fixed point definitions of the exponentials are better behaving when digging and contraction are discarded. On the other hand, the co-absorption rule cannot be derived in
\mathsf{LL}
, and so it prevents parsimonious logic becoming a genuine subsystem of the latter. This led the authors of the present paper to introduce
parsimonious linear logic
, a subsystem of linear logic (in particular, co-absorption-free) that nonetheless allows a stream-based interpretation of the exponentials.

We present two finitary proof systems for parsimonious linear logic: the system
\mathsf{nu}\mathsf{PLL}
, supporting non-uniform exponentials, and
\mathsf{PLL}
, a fully uniform version. We investigate non-wellfounded counterparts of
\mathsf{nu}\mathsf{PLL}
and
\mathsf{PLL}
, adapting to our setting the progressing criterion to maintain logical consistency.
To recover the proof-theoretical behavior of
\mathsf{nu}\mathsf{PLL}
and
\mathsf{PLL}
, we identify further global conditions on non-wellfounded proofs, that is, some forms of regularity to capture the notions of uniformity and non-uniformity.
This leads us to two main non-wellfounded proof systems:
regular parsimonious linear logic
(
\mathsf{r}\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
), defined via the regularity condition and corresponding to
\mathsf{PLL}
, and
weakly regular parsimonious linear logic
(
\mathsf{wr}\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
), defined via a
weak regularity
condition and corresponding to
\mathsf{nu}\mathsf{PLL}
.

The major contribution of this paper is the study of continuous cut-elimination in the setting of non-wellfounded parsimonious linear logic. We first introduce Scott-domains of partially defined non-wellfounded proofs, ordered by an approximation relation.
Then, we define special infinitary proof rewriting strategies called
maximal and continuous infinitary cut-elimination strategies
(
\mathsf{mc}\text{-}\mathsf{ices}
) which compute (Scott-)continuous functions.
Productivity in this framework is established by showing that, in presence of a good global condition (progressing, regularity or weak regularity), these continuous functions return totally defined cut-free non-wellfounded proofs and preserve the global condition: progressing (
Theorem

40
), and regularity or weak regularity (
Theorem

48
).

On a technical side, we stress that our methods and results distinguish from previous approaches to cut-elimination in a non-wellfounded setting in many respects.
First,
we get rid of many technical notions typically introduced to prove infinitary cut-elimination,
such as
the
multicut rule

or the

fairness conditions
(as in, e.g.,
[
18
,
5
]
), as these notions are subsumed by a
finitary approximation
approach to cut-elimination.
Furthermore, we prove productivity of cut-elimination and preservation of progressiveness in a more direct and constructive way, i.e., without going through auxiliary proof systems and avoiding arguments by contradiction (see, e.g.,
[
5
]
).
Finally, we prove for the first time preservation of regularity properties under continuous cut-elimination, essentially exploiting methods for compressing transfinite rewriting sequences to
\omega
-long ones from
[
35
,
24
,
32
]
.

Finally, we define a denotational semantics for non-wellfounded parsimonious logic based on the relational model, with a standard multiset-based interpretation of the exponentials, and we show that this semantics is preserved under continuous cut-elimination (
Theorem

56
).
We also prove that
extending non-wellfounded parsimonious linear logic with digging
prevents the existence of a cut-elimination result preserving the semantics (
Theorem

58
).
Therefore, the impossibility of a stream-based definition of
\oc
that validates digging (and contraction).

2
Preliminary notions

In this section we recall some basic notions from (non-wellfounded) proof theory, fixing the notation that will be adopted in this paper.

2.1
Derivations and coderivations

We assume that the reader is familiar with the syntax of sequent calculus, e.g.
[
36
]
.
Here we specify some conventions adopted to simplify the content of this paper.

We consider (
sequent
)
rules
of the form

\scriptstyle\mathsf{r}\;

\Gamma

or

\Gamma_{1}

\scriptstyle\mathsf{r}\;

\Gamma

or

\Gamma_{1}

\Gamma_{2}

\scriptstyle\mathsf{r}\;

\Gamma

,
and we refer
to the sequents
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
as the
premises
,
and
to the sequent
\Gamma
as the
conclusion
of the rule
\mathsf{r}
.
To avoid technicalities of the sequents-as-lists presentation,
we follow
[
5
]

and we consider
sequents
as
sets of occurrences of formulas
from a given set of formulas.
In particular, when we refer to a formula in a sequent we always consider a
specific occurrence
of it.

Definition 1
.

A (binary, possibly infinite)
tree

\mathcal{T}
is a subset of words in
\{1,2\}^{*}

that contains the empty word
\epsilon
(the
root
of
\mathcal{T}
)
and is
ordered-prefix-closed

(i.e., if
n\in\{1,2\}
and
vn\in\mathcal{T}
, then
v\in\mathcal{T}
,
and
if moreover
v2\in\mathcal{T}
, then
v1\in\mathcal{T}
).
The elements of
\mathcal{T}
are called
nodes
and their
height
is the length of the word.
A
child
of
v\in\mathcal{T}
is any
vn\in\mathcal{T}
with
n\in\{1,2\}
.
The
prefix order
is a partial order
\leq_{\mathcal{T}}
on
\mathcal{T}
defined by: for any
v,v^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}
,
v\leq_{\mathcal{T}}v^{\prime}
if
v^{\prime}=vw
for some
w\in\{1,2\}^{*}
.
A maximal element of
\leq_{\mathcal{T}}
is a
leaf
of
\mathcal{T}
.
A
branch
of
\mathcal{T}
is a set
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{T}
such that
\epsilon\in\mathcal{B}
and if
w\in\mathcal{B}
is not a leaf of
\mathcal{T}
then
w
has exactly one child in
\mathcal{B}
.

A
coderivation
over a set of rules
\mathcal{S}

is a labeling
\mathcal{D}
of a tree
\mathcal{T}
by sequents such that
if
v
is a node of
\mathcal{T}
with children
v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}
(with
n\in\{0,1,2\}
),
then there is an occurrence of a rule
\mathsf{r}
in
\mathcal{S}
with conclusion the sequent
\mathcal{D}(v)
and premises the sequents
\mathcal{D}(v_{1}),\ldots,\mathcal{D}(v_{n})
.
The
height
of
\mathsf{r}
in
\mathcal{D}
is the height of the node
v\in\mathcal{T}
such that
\mathcal{D}(v)
is the conclusion of
\mathsf{r}
.

The
conclusion
of
\mathcal{D}
is the sequent
\mathcal{D}(\epsilon)
.
If
v
is a node of the tree, the
sub-coderivation
of
\mathcal{D}
rooted at
v
is the coderivation
\mathcal{D}_{v}
defined by
\mathcal{D}_{v}(w)=\mathcal{D}(vw)
.

A coderivation
\mathcal{D}
is
\mathsf{r}
-free
(for a rule
\mathsf{r}\!\in\!\mathcal{S}
) if it contains no occurrence of
\mathsf{r}
.
It is
regular
if it has finitely many distinct sub-coderivations; it is
non-wellfounded
if it labels an infinite tree, and
it is a
derivation
(with
size

|\mathcal{D}|\in\mathbb{N}
) if it labels a finite tree (with
|\mathcal{D}|
nodes).

Given a set of coderivations
\mathsf{X}
, a sequent
\Gamma
is
provable
in
\mathsf{X}
(noted
\vdash_{\mathsf{X}}\Gamma
)
if there is a coderivation in
\mathsf{X}
with conclusion
\Gamma
.

While derivations are usually represented as finite trees, regular coderivations can be represented as
finite
directed (possibly cyclic) graphs: a cycle is created by linking the roots of two identical subcoderivations.

Definition 2
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a coderivation labeling a tree
\mathcal{T}
.
A
bar
(resp.
prebar
) of
\mathcal{D}
is a set
\mathcal{V}\subseteq\mathcal{T}
where:

•

any branch (resp. infinite branch) of the tree
\mathcal{T}
underlying
\mathcal{D}
contains a node in
\mathcal{V}
;

•

any pair of nodes in
\mathcal{V}
are mutually incomparable with respect to the prefix order
\leq_{\mathcal{T}}
.

The
height
of a prebar
\mathcal{V}
of
\mathcal{D}
is the minimal height of the nodes of
\mathcal{V}
.

3
Parsimonious Linear Logic

In this paper we consider the set of
formulas
for propositional multiplicative-exponential linear logic with units (
\mathsf{MELL}
).
These are generated by a countable set of propositional variables
\mathcal{A}=\{X,Y,\ldots\}
using the following grammar:

A,B\Coloneqq X\mid{X}^{\bot}\mid A\mathop{\otimes}B\mid A\invamp B\mid\oc A\mid\wn A\mid\mathsf{1}\mid\bot

A
\oc
-formula
(resp.
\wn
-formula
) is a formula of the form
\oc A
(resp.
\wn A
).

Linear negation

{(\cdot)}^{\bot}
is defined by De Morgan’s laws

{({{A}^{\bot}})}^{\bot}=A
,

{(A\mathop{\otimes}B)}^{\bot}={A}^{\bot}\invamp{B}^{\bot}
,

{(\oc A)}^{\bot}=\wn{A}^{\bot}
, and

{(\mathsf{1})}^{\bot}=\bot

while
linear implication
is defined as
A\multimap B\coloneqq{A}^{\bot}\invamp B
.

Figure 1:
Sequent calculus rules of
\mathsf{PLL}
.

Definition 3
.

Parsimonious linear logic
, denoted by
\mathsf{PLL}
, is the set of rules in

Figure

1
, that is,

axiom
(
\mathsf{ax}
),

cut
(
\mathsf{cut}
),

tensor
(
\mathop{\otimes}
),

par
(
\invamp
),

one
(
\mathsf{1}
),

bottom
(
\bot
),

functorial promotion
(
\mathsf{f\mathsf{\oc p}}
),

weakening
(
\wn\mathsf{w}
),

absorption
(
\wn\mathsf{b}
).
Rules

\mathsf{ax}
,
\mathop{\otimes}
,
\invamp
,
\mathsf{1}
and
\bot

are called
multiplicative
, while rules
\mathsf{f\mathsf{\oc p}}
,
\wn\mathsf{w}
and
\wn\mathsf{b}
are called
exponential
. We also denote by
\mathsf{PLL}
the set of derivations over the rules in
\mathsf{PLL}
.

\begin{array}[]{c|c|c|c}\underline{0}&\underline{1}&\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{abs}}&\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{der}}\\

2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}\kern 6.70834pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 18.04025pt\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\wn{A}^{\bot}\invamp A$}\kern 0.0pt$}}\kern 6.70834pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\end{array}

Figure 2:
Examples of derivations in
\mathsf{PLL}
.

Example 4
.

Figure

2
gives some examples of derivation in
\mathsf{PLL}
.
The (distinct) derivations
\underline{0}
and
\underline{1}
prove the same formula
\mathbf{N}\coloneqq\oc(X\multimap X)\multimap X\multimap X
.
The derivation
\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{abs}}

proves the

absorption law

\oc A\multimap A\mathop{\otimes}\oc A
;
the derivation
\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{der}}

proves
the
dereliction law

\oc A\multimap A
.

The
cut-elimination
relation
\to_{\mathsf{cut}}
in
\mathsf{PLL}

is the union of

principal
cut-elimination steps
in

Figure

3
(
multiplicative
)
and

Figure

4
(
exponential
)
and

commutative
cut-elimination steps in
Figure

5
. The reflexive-transitive closure of
\to_{\mathsf{cut}}
is noted
\to_{\mathsf{cut}}^{*}
.

Theorem 5
.

For every
\mathcal{D}\in\mathsf{PLL}
,
there is a cut-free
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\in\mathsf{PLL}
such that
\mathcal{D}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}^{*}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
.

Sketch of proof.

We associate with any derivation
\mathcal{D}
in
\mathsf{PLL}

a derivation
\mathcal{D}^{\spadesuit}

in
\mathsf{MELL}
sequent calculus.
Thanks to additional commutative cut-elimination steps,
we prove that cut-elimination in
\mathsf{MELL}
rewrites
\mathcal{D}^{\spadesuit}
to the translation of
a derivation in
\mathsf{PLL}
.
The termination of cut-elimination in
\mathsf{PLL}
follows from strong normalisation of (second-order)
\mathsf{MELL}

[
29
]
.
∎

Akin to light linear logic
[
20
,
22
,
31
]
, the exponential rules of
\mathsf{PLL}
are weaker than those in
\mathsf{MELL}
: the usual promotion rule is replaced by
\mathsf{f\mathsf{\oc p}}
(
functorial promotion
), and the usual contraction and dereliction rules by
\wn\mathsf{b}
.
As a consequence, the
digging
formula
\oc A\multimap\oc\oc A
and the
contraction
formula
\oc A\multimap\oc A\otimes\oc A
are not provable in
\mathsf{PLL}
(unlike the dereliction formula,
Example

4
).
This allows us to interpret computationally these weaker exponentials in terms of streams, as well as to control the complexity of cut-elimination
[
25
,
26
]
.

It is easy to show that
\mathsf{MELL}=\mathsf{PLL}+\text{digging}
: if we add the digging formula as an axiom (or equivalently, the
digging rule

\mathsf{\wn\wn d}
in
Figure

13
) to the set of rules in
Figure

1
, then the contraction formula becomes provable, and the obtained proof system coincides with
\mathsf{MELL}
.

Figure 6:
Exponential cut-elimination steps in
\mathsf{nu}\mathsf{PLL}
.

4
Non-wellfounded Parsimonious Linear Logic

In linear logic, a formula
\oc A
is interpreted as the availability of
A
at will.
This intuition still holds in
\mathsf{PLL}
. Indeed,
the Curry-Howard correspondence interprets rule
\mathsf{f\mathsf{\oc p}}

introducing the modality
\oc

as an operator taking a derivation
\mathcal{D}
of
A
and
creating a (infinite)
stream

({\mathcal{D}},{\mathcal{D}},\ldots,\allowbreak{\mathcal{D}},\ldots)

of copies of the proof
\mathcal{D}
.
Each element of the stream is accessed via the cut-elimination step
\mathsf{f\mathsf{\oc p}}
vs
\wn\mathsf{b}
in
Figure

4
:
rule
\wn\mathsf{b}

is interpreted as an operator
popping
one copy of
\mathcal{D}
out of the stream. Pushing these ideas further, Mazza
[
25
]
introduced
parsimonious logic

\mathbf{PL}
, a type system (comprising rules
\mathsf{f\mathsf{\oc p}}
and
\wn\mathsf{b}
) characterizing the logspace decidable problems.

Mazza and Terui then introduced in
[
26
]
another type system,
\mathbf{nuPL}_{\forall\ell}
, based on parsimonious logic
and capturing the complexity class
\mathbf{P}/\mathsf{poly}
(i.e., the problems decidable by polynomial size families of Boolean circuits
[
4
]
). Their system is endowed with a
non-uniform
version of the functorial promotion, which takes a finite set of proofs
\mathcal{D}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{D}_{n}
of
A
and a (possibly non-recursive) function
f\colon\mathbb{N}\to\{1,\ldots,n\}
as premises, and constructs a proof of
\oc A
modeling the stream
({\mathcal{D}_{f(0)}},{\mathcal{D}_{f(1)}},\ldots,\allowbreak{\mathcal{D}_{f(n)}},\ldots)
. This typing rule is the key tool to encode the so-called
advices
for Turing machines, an essential step to show completeness for
\mathbf{P}/\mathsf{poly}
.

In a similar vein, we can endow
\mathsf{PLL}
with a non-uniform version of
\mathsf{f\mathsf{\oc p}}
called
infinitely branching promotion
(
\mathsf{ib}\mathsf{\oc p}
), which constructs a
stream
({\mathcal{D}_{0}},{\mathcal{D}_{1}},\ldots,\allowbreak{\mathcal{D}_{n}},\ldots)
with finite support, i.e.,
made of
finitely
many distinct derivations (of the same conclusion):
2
2

2

Rule
\mathsf{ib}\mathsf{\oc p}
is reminiscent of the
\omega
-rule used in (first-order) Peano arithmetic to derive formulas of the form
\forall x\phi
that cannot be proven in a uniform way.

(1)

The side condition on
\mathsf{ib}\mathsf{\oc p}
provides a proof theoretic counterpart to the function
f\colon\mathbb{N}\to\{1,\ldots,n\}
in
\mathbf{nuPL}_{\forall\ell}
.
Clearly,
\mathsf{f\mathsf{\oc p}}
is subsumed by the rule
\mathsf{ib}\mathsf{\oc p}
, as it corresponds to the special (uniform) case where
\mathcal{D}_{i}=\mathcal{D}_{i+1}
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
.

Definition 6
.

We define the set of rules
\mathsf{nu}\mathsf{PLL}\coloneqq\{\mathsf{ax},\mathop{\otimes},\invamp,\mathsf{1},\bot,\mathsf{cut},\wn\mathsf{b},\wn\mathsf{w},\mathsf{ib}\mathsf{\oc p}\}
.
We also denote by
\mathsf{nu}\mathsf{PLL}
the set of derivations over the rules in
\mathsf{nu}\mathsf{PLL}
.
3
3

3

To be rigorous, this requires a slight change in
Definition

1
: the tree labeled by a derivation in
\mathsf{nu}\mathsf{PLL}
must be over
\mathbb{N}^{\omega}
instead of
\{1,2\}^{*}
, in order to deal with infinitely branching derivations.

There are some notable differences between
\mathsf{nu}\mathsf{PLL}
and Mazza and Terui’s original system
~\mathbf{nuPL}_{\forall\ell}

[
26
]
.
As opposed to
\mathsf{nu}\mathsf{PLL}
,
\mathbf{nuPL}_{\forall\ell}

is formulated as an intuitionistic (type) system. Furthermore, to achieve completeness for
\mathbf{P}/\mathsf{poly}
, these authors introduced second-order quantifiers and the co-absorption (
\oc\mathsf{b}
) and co-weakening (
\oc\mathsf{w}
) rules displayed in (
1
).

Cut-elimination
steps for
\mathsf{nu}\mathsf{PLL}
are
in
Figures

3
,
5
and
6
.
In particular, the step
\mathsf{ib}\mathsf{\oc p}\mbox{-vs-}\wn\mathsf{b}
in
Figure

6

pops
the first derivation
\mathcal{D}_{0}
of
\mathsf{ib}\mathsf{\oc p}
out of the
stream
({\mathcal{D}_{0}},{\mathcal{D}_{1}},\ldots,\allowbreak{\mathcal{D}_{n}},\ldots)
.

4.1
From infinitely branching proofs to non-wellfounded proofs

In this paper we explore a dual approach to the one of
\mathbf{nuPL}_{\forall\ell}
(and
\mathsf{nu}\mathsf{PLL}
):
instead of considering (wellfounded) derivations with infinite branching,
we consider (non-wellfounded) coderivations with finite branching.
For this purpose,
the infinitary rule
\mathsf{ib}\mathsf{\oc p}
of
\mathsf{nu}\mathsf{PLL}
is replaced by the binary rule below, called
conditional promotion
(
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
):

\Gamma,A

\wn\Gamma,\oc A

\scriptstyle\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\;

\wn\Gamma,\oc A

Two non-wellfounded and non-progressing coderivations in
\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}

Definition 7
.

We define the set of rules
\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}\coloneqq\{\mathsf{ax},\mathop{\otimes},\invamp,\mathsf{1},\bot,\mathsf{cut},\wn\mathsf{b},\wn\mathsf{w},\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\}
.
We also denote by
\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
the set of coderivations over the rules in
\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
.

In other words,
\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
is the set of coderivations generated by the same rules as
\mathsf{PLL}
, except that
\mathsf{f\mathsf{\oc p}}
is replaced by
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
.
From now on, we will only consider coderivations in
\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
.

Example 8
.

Figure

7
shows two non-wellfounded coderivations in
\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
:

\mathcal{D}_{\!\lightning}
(resp.
\mathcal{D}_{\wn}
) has an infinite branch of
\mathsf{cut}
(resp.
\wn\mathsf{b}
) rules, and is (resp. is not) regular.

We can embed
\mathsf{PLL}
and
\mathsf{nu}\mathsf{PLL}
into
\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
via the conclusion-preserving translations
{(\cdot)}^{\circ}\colon\mathsf{PLL}\to\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
and
{(\cdot)}^{\bullet}\colon\mathsf{nu}\mathsf{PLL}\to\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
defined in
Figure

8
by induction on derivations: they map all rules to themselves except
\mathsf{f\mathsf{\oc p}}
and
\mathsf{ib}\mathsf{\oc p}
, which are “unpacked” into non-wellfounded coderivations that iterate infinitely many times the rule
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
.

2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to70.56111pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 36.43335pt\hbox{\hbox{$\reflectbox{$\ddots$}$}}\kern 31.53055pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 4.9028pt\hbox \mbox{for all }\mathsf{r}\in\{\invamp,\bot,\wn\mathsf{w},\wn\mathsf{b}\}\mbox{ and }\mathsf{t}\in\{\mathsf{cut},\mathop{\otimes}\}\mbox{ ($\mathsf{ax}$ and $\mathsf{1}$ are translated by themselves).}\end{array}

Figure 9:
A non-wellfounded box in
\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
.

Definition 9
.

A
non-wellfounded box

(
nwb
for short)
is a coderivation
\mathcal{D}\in\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}

with an infinite branch
\{\epsilon,2,22,\dots\}

(the
main branch
of
\mathcal{D}
) all labeled by
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
rules as in
Figure

9
,
where
\oc A
in the conclusion is the
principal formula
of
\mathcal{D}
, and
\mathcal{D}_{0},\mathcal{D}_{1},\ldots
are the
calls
of
\mathcal{D}
.
We denote
\mathcal{D}
by
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}_{(\mathcal{D}_{0},\ldots,\mathcal{D}_{n},\ldots)}
.

Let
\mathfrak{S}=\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}_{(\mathcal{D}_{0},\ldots,\mathcal{D}_{n},\ldots)}
be a
nwb
.
We may write
\mathfrak{S}(i)
to denote
\mathcal{D}_{i}
.
We say that
\mathfrak{S}
has
finite support
(resp. is
periodic
with
period

k
)
if
\{\mathfrak{S}(i)\mid i\in\mathbb{N}\}
is finite (resp. if
\mathfrak{S}(i)=\mathfrak{S}(k+i)
for any
i\in\mathbb{N}
).
A coderivation
\mathcal{D}

has
finite support
(resp. is
periodic
)
if any
nwb
in
\mathcal{D}
has finite support (resp. is periodic).

Example 10
.

The only
\mathsf{cut}
-free derivations of the formula
\mathbf{N}\coloneqq\oc(X\multimap X)\multimap X\multimap X
are of the form
\underline{n}
below on the right, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, up to permutations of the rules
\wn\mathsf{w}
and
\mathop{\otimes}
(the derivations
\underline{0}
and
\underline{1}
in
Example

4
are special cases of it)

to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 16.20638pt\hbox{\hbox{$\kern to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 1.8pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\wn\mathsf{b}\times n\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to1.8pt{\vfill\hbox to158.44447pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.0pt\hbox to158.44447pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 1.8pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.43518pt\kern-0.7pt\hbox{\kern 58.17857pt\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\wn(X\otimes{X\!}^{\bot}),{X\!}^{\bot}\!,X$}\kern 0.0pt$}}\kern 41.9722pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\kern 1.43518pt\kern-0.7pt\hbox{\kern 58.60857pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 1.8pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\invamp\times 2\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to1.8pt{\vfill\hbox to88.38882pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.0pt\hbox to88.38882pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 1.8pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}\kern 35.02782pt}\kern 1.43518pt\kern-0.7pt\hbox{\kern 58.60857pt\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\wn(X\otimes{X\!}^{\bot})\invamp{X\!}^{\bot}\invamp X$}\kern 0.0pt$}}\kern 35.02782pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\quad

(3)

Consider the
nwb

\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}_{(\underline{i_{0}},\ldots,\underline{i_{n}},\ldots)}
above on the left, proving the formula
\oc\mathbf{N}
, where
i_{j}\in\{0,1\}
for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
.
Thus
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}_{(\underline{i_{0}},\ldots,\underline{i_{n}},\ldots)}
has finite support, as its only calls can be
\underline{0}
or
\underline{1}
,
and it is periodic if and only if so is the infinite sequence
(i_{0},\ldots,i_{n},\ldots)\in\{0,1\}^{\omega}
.

The
cut-elimination
steps
\to_{\mathsf{cut}}
for
\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
are in
Figures

3
,
5
and
10
.
Computationally, they allow the
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
rule to be interpreted as a
coinductive
definition of a stream of type
\oc A
from a stream of the same type to which an element of type
A
is prepended.
In particular, the cut-elimination step
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
vs
\wn\mathsf{b}
accesses the head of a stream:
rule
\wn\mathsf{b}

acts as a
pop
operator.

As a consequence, the
nwb
in
Figure

9
constructs a stream
({\mathcal{D}_{0}},{\mathcal{D}_{1}},\ldots,\allowbreak{\mathcal{D}_{n}},\ldots)
similarly to
\mathsf{ib}\mathsf{\oc p}
but, unlike the latter, all the
\mathcal{D}_{i}
’s may be pairwise distinct.
The reader expert in linear logic can see a
nwb
as a box with possibly
infinitely many
distinct contents (its calls), while usual linear logic boxes (and
\mathsf{f\mathsf{\oc p}}
in
\mathsf{PLL}
) provide infinitely many copies of the
same
content.

Rules
\mathsf{f\mathsf{\oc p}}
in
\mathsf{PLL}
and
\mathsf{ib}\mathsf{\oc p}
in
\mathsf{nu}\mathsf{PLL}
are mapped by
{(\cdot)}^{\circ}
and
{(\cdot)}^{\bullet}
into
nwb
s, which are non-wellfounded coderivations.
Hence, the cut-elimination steps
\mathsf{f\mathsf{\oc p}}
vs
\mathsf{f\mathsf{\oc p}}
in
\mathsf{PLL}
and
\mathsf{ib}\mathsf{\oc p}
vs
\mathsf{ib}\mathsf{\oc p}
in
\mathsf{nu}\mathsf{PLL}
can only be simulated by infinitely many cut-elimination steps in
\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
.

Note that
\mathcal{D}_{\!\lightning}\in\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
in
Figure

7
is not
\mathsf{cut}
-free, and
if
\mathcal{D}_{\!\lightning}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathcal{D}
then
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}_{\!\lightning}
: thus
\mathcal{D}_{\!\lightning}
cannot reduce to a
\mathsf{cut}
-free coderivation, and so the cut-elimination theorem fails in
\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
.

Figure 10:
Exponential cut-elimination steps for coderivations of
\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
.

4.2
Consistency via a progressing criterion

In a non-wellfounded setting such as
\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}\!
, any sequent is provable.
Indeed, the (non-wellfounded) coderivation
\mathcal{D}_{\!\lightning}
in
Figure

7
shows that any non-empty sequent (in particular, any formula) is provable in
\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
,
and the empty sequent is provable in
\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
by applying the
\mathsf{cut}
rule on the conclusions
B
and
{B}^{\bot}
(for any formula
B
) of two derivations
\mathcal{D}_{\!\lightning}
.

The standard way to recover logical consistency in non-wellfounded proof theory is to introduce
a global soundness condition on coderivations, called
progressing criterion
.
In
\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
, this criterion relies on tracking occurrences of
\oc
-formulas in a coderivation.

Definition 11
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a coderivation in
\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
.
It is
weakly progressing
if every infinite branch contains infinitely many right premises of
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
-rules.

An occurrence of a formula in a premise of a rule
\mathsf{r}
is the
parent
of an occurrence of a formula in the conclusion if they are connected according to the edges depicted in
Figure

11
.

A
\oc
-thread
(resp.
\wn
-thread
) in
\mathcal{D}

is a maximal sequence
(A_{i})_{i\in I}
of
\oc
-formulas (resp.
\wn
-formulas)
for some downward-closed
I\subseteq\mathbb{N}
such that
A_{i+1}
is the parent of
A_{i}
for all
i\in I
.
A
\oc
-thread
(A_{i})_{i\in I}
is
progressing
if
A_{j}
is in the conclusion of a
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
for infinitely many
j\in I
.

\mathcal{D}
is
progressing
if every infinite branch contains a progressing
\oc
-thread.
We define
\mathsf{p}\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
(resp.
\mathsf{w}\mathsf{p}\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
) as the set of progressing (resp. weak-progressing) coderivations in
\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
.

Figure 11:
\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
rules: edges connect a formula in the conclusion with its parent(s) in a premise.

Remark 12
.

Clearly, any progressing coderivation is weakly progressing too, but the converse fails (
Example

14
), therefore
\mathsf{p}\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}\subsetneq\mathsf{w}\mathsf{p}\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
.
Moreover, the main branch of any
nwb
contains by definition a progressing
\oc
-thread of its principal formula.

Remark 13
.

Any branch
\mathcal{B}
in a progressing coderivation
\mathcal{D}
contains at most (and hence exactly) one progressing
\oc
-thread.
This follows by maximality of
\oc
-threads and the fact that conclusions of
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
rules contain at most one
\oc
-formula.
As a consequence, any infinite
\oc
-thread
\tau
of a branch
\mathcal{B}
in a progressing coderivation
\mathcal{D}
must be progressing.

Example 14
.

Coderivations in
Figure

7
are not weakly progressing (hence, not progressing):
the rightmost branch of
\mathcal{D}_{\!\lightning}
, i.e., the branch
\{\epsilon,2,22,\ldots\}
,
and the unique branch of
\mathcal{D}_{\wn}

are infinite and contain no
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
-rules.
In contrast, the
nwb

\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}_{(\underline{i_{0}},\ldots,\underline{i_{n}},\ldots)}
in
Example

10
is progressing by
Remark

12
, since its main branch is the only infinite branch.
Below, a regular, weakly progressing but not progressing coderivation (
\oc X
in the conclusion of
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
is a cut formula, so the branch
\{\epsilon,2,21,212,2121,\dots\}
is infinite but has no progressing
\oc
-thread).

Lemma 15
.

Let
\Gamma
be a sequent.
Then,
\vdash_{\mathsf{PLL}}\Gamma
if and only if
\vdash_{\mathsf{w}\mathsf{p}\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}}\Gamma
.

Proof.

Given
\mathcal{D}\in\mathsf{PLL}
,
{\mathcal{D}}^{\circ}\in\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
preserves the conclusion and is progressing, hence weakly progressing (see
Remark

12
).
Conversely, given a weakly progressing coderivation
\mathcal{D}
,
we define
a derivation
\mathcal{D}^{f}\in\mathsf{PLL}
with the same conclusion
by applying, bottom-up, the translation:

{\left(\!{{{}{}{}}\vbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$$}}\kern 10.00002pt}\hbox{\hbox{$\hskip 8.0pt$}}\kern 10.00002pt}\hbox{\hbox{$$}}}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$$}}\vbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\kern to25.69446pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}\kern 1.1528pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 1.1528pt\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\wn\Gamma,\oc A$}\kern 0.0pt$}}\kern 1.1528pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}

with
\mathsf{r}\neq\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
.
Note that the derivation
\mathcal{D}^{f}
is well-defined because
\mathcal{D}
is weakly progressing.
∎

Corollary 16
.

The empty sequent is not provable in
\mathsf{w}\mathsf{p}\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
(and hence in
\mathsf{p}\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
).

Proof.

If the empty sequent were provable in
\mathsf{w}\mathsf{p}\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
, then there would be a cut-free derivation
\mathcal{D}\in\mathsf{PLL}
of the empty sequent by
Lemmas

15
and
5
, but this is impossible since
\mathsf{cut}
is the only rule in
\mathsf{PLL}
that could have the empty sequent in its conclusion.
∎

4.3
Recovering (weak forms of) regularity

The progressing criterion cannot capture the finiteness condition of the rule
\mathsf{ib}\mathsf{\oc p}
in the derivations in
\mathsf{nu}\mathsf{PLL}
.
By means of example, consider the
nwb

below, which is progressing but cannot be the image of
the rule
\mathsf{ib}\mathsf{\oc p}
via
{(\cdot)}^{\bullet}
(see
Figure

8
) since
\{\mathcal{D}_{i}\mid i\in\mathbb{N}\}
is infinite.

\textstyle{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}_{0}}

\oc\mathbf{N}

\textstyle{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}_{1}}

\oc\mathbf{N}

\textstyle{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}_{n}}

\oc\mathbf{N}

\vdots

\scriptstyle\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\;

{\oc\oc\mathbf{N}}

\scriptstyle\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\;

\ddots

\scriptstyle\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\;

{\oc\oc\mathbf{N}}

\scriptstyle\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\;

\oc\oc\mathbf{N}

with

\mathcal{D}_{i}=\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}_{(\underbrace{{\scriptstyle{\underline{1},\ldots,\underline{1}}}}_{i},\underline{0},\ldots)}

for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
.

(4)

To identify in
\mathsf{p}\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
the coderivations corresponding to derivations in
\mathsf{nu}\mathsf{PLL}
and in
\mathsf{PLL}
via the translations
{(\cdot)}^{\bullet}
and
{(\cdot)}^{\circ}
, respectively, we need additional conditions.

Definition 17
.

A coderivation is

weakly regular
if it has only finitely many distinct sub-coderivations
whose conclusions are left premises of
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
-rules;
it is

finitely expandable
if any branch contains finitely many
\mathsf{cut}
and
\wn\mathsf{b}
rules.
We denote by
\mathsf{wr}\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
(resp.
\mathsf{r}\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
) the set of
weakly regular (resp. regular) and finitely expandable coderivations in
\mathsf{p}\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
.

Remark 18
.

Regularity implies weak regularity and the converse fails as
shown in
Example

19
below, so
\mathsf{r}\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}\subsetneq\mathsf{wr}\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
. Given
\mathcal{D}\in\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
progressing and finitely expandable, it is regular (resp. weakly regular)
if and only if any
nwb
in
\mathcal{D}
is periodic (resp. has finite support).

Example 19
.

Coderivations
\mathcal{D}_{\!\lightning}
and
\mathcal{D}_{\wn}
in
Figure

7
are not finitely expandable, as their infinite branch has infinitely many
\mathsf{cut}
or
\wn\mathsf{b}
,
but they are weakly regular, since they have no
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
rules.
The coderivation in (
4
) is not weakly regular because
\{\mathcal{D}_{i}\mid i\in\mathbb{N}\}
is infinite.

An example of a weakly regular but not regular coderivation is the
nwb

\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}_{(\underline{i_{0}},\ldots,\underline{i_{n}},\ldots)}
in
Example

10
when the infinite sequence

(i_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\in\{0,1\}^{\omega}
is not periodic:
\underline{0}
and
\underline{1}
are the only coderivations ending in the left premise of a
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
rule (so the
nwb
is weakly regular), but there are infinitely many distinct coderivations ending in the right premise of a
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
rule (so the
nwb
is not regular).
Moreover, that
nwb
is finitely expandable, as it contains no
\wn\mathsf{b}
or
\mathsf{cut}
.

The sets
\mathsf{r}\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
and
\mathsf{wr}\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
are the non-wellfounded counterparts of
\mathsf{PLL}
and
\mathsf{nu}\mathsf{PLL}
, respectively.
Indeed, we have the following correspondence via the translations
{(\cdot)}^{\circ}
and
{(\cdot)}^{\bullet}
.

Proposition 20
.

The following statements hold.

1.

If
\mathcal{D}\in\mathsf{PLL}
(resp.
\mathcal{D}\in\mathsf{nu}\mathsf{PLL}
) with conclusion
\Gamma
, then
{\mathcal{D}}^{\circ}\in\mathsf{r}\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
(resp.
{\mathcal{D}}^{\bullet}\!\in\mathsf{wr}\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
) with conclusion
\Gamma
, and every
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
in
{\mathcal{D}}^{\circ}
(resp.
{\mathcal{D}}^{\bullet}
) belongs to a
nwb
.

2.

If
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\in\mathsf{r}\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
(resp.
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\in\mathsf{wr}\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
) and every
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
belongs to a
nwb
, then there is
\mathcal{D}\in\mathsf{PLL}
(resp.
\mathcal{D}\in\mathsf{nu}\mathsf{PLL}
) such that
{\mathcal{D}}^{\circ}=\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
(resp.
{\mathcal{D}}^{\bullet}=\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
).

1.

By straightforward induction on
\mathcal{D}\in\mathsf{PLL}
(resp.
\mathcal{D}\in\mathsf{nu}\mathsf{PLL}
).

2.

\mathbf{d}(\mathcal{D})\in\mathbb{N}
by
Lemma

45
. We can then prove the statement by induction on
\mathbf{d}(\mathcal{D})
. We prove the statement by induction on the measure
\mathbf{d}(\mathcal{D})
(see
Definition

42
and
Lemma

45
).

Progressing and weak progressing coincide in finitely expandable coderivations.

Lemma 21
.

Let
\mathcal{D}\in\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
be finitely expandable. If
\mathcal{D}\in\mathsf{w}\mathsf{p}\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
then any infinite branch contains the main branch of a
nwb
. Moreover,
\mathcal{D}\in\mathsf{p}\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
if and only if
\mathcal{D}\in\mathsf{w}\mathsf{p}\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{D}\in\mathsf{w}\mathsf{p}\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
be finitely expandable, and let
\mathcal{B}
be an infinite branch in
\mathcal{D}
. By finite expandability there is
h\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\mathcal{B}
contains no conclusion of a
\mathsf{cut}
or
\wn\mathsf{b}
with height greater than
h
.
Moreover, by weakly progressing there is an infinite sequence
h\leq h_{0}<h_{1}<\ldots<h_{n}<\ldots
such that the sequent of
\mathcal{B}
at height
h_{i}
has shape
\wn\Gamma_{i},\oc A_{i}
. By inspecting the rules in
Figure

1
, each such
\wn\Gamma_{i},\oc A_{i}
can be the conclusion of either a
\wn\mathsf{w}
or a
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
(with right premise
\wn\Gamma_{i},\oc A_{i}
). So, there is a
k
large enough such that, for any
i\geq k
, only the latter case applies (and, in particular,
\Gamma_{i}=\Gamma
and
A_{i}=A
for some
\Gamma,A
). Therefore,
h_{k}
is the root of a
nwb
. This also shows
\mathcal{D}\in\mathsf{p}\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
. By
Remark

12
,
\mathsf{p}\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}\subseteq\mathsf{w}\mathsf{p}\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
.
∎

By inspecting the steps in
Figures

5
,
3
and
10
, we prove the following preservations.

Proposition 22
.

Cut elimination preserves weak-regularity, regularity and finite expandability.
Therefore, if
\mathcal{D}\in\mathsf{X}
with
\mathsf{X}\in\{\mathsf{r}\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty},\mathsf{wr}\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}\}
and

\mathcal{D}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
, then also
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\in\mathsf{X}
.

Proof.

By inspection of the cut-elimination steps defined in
Figures

5
,
3
and
10
.
∎

5
Continuous cut-elimination

Cut-elimination for (finitary) sequent calculi proceeds by introducing a proof rewriting strategy that stepwise decreases an appropriate termination ordering (see, e.g,
[
36
]
). Typically, these proof rewriting strategies consist on pushing upward the topmost cuts via the cut-elimination steps in order to eventually eliminate them.

A somewhat dual approach is investigated in the context of non-wellfounded proofs
[
5
,
18
]
. It consists on
infinitary
proof rewriting strategies that gradually push upward the bottommost cuts. In this setting, the progressing condition is essential to guarantee
productivity
, i.e., that such proof rewriting strategies construct
strictly increasing approximations of the cut-free proof, which can thus be obtained as a (well-defined)
limit
.

A major obstacle of this approach arises when the bottommost cut
\mathsf{r}
is below another one
\mathsf{r}^{\prime}
. In this case, no cut-elimination step can be applied to
\mathsf{r}
, so proof rewriting runs into an apparent stumbling block. To circumvent this problem, in
[
5
,
18
]
a special cut-elimination step is introduced, which merges
\mathsf{r}
and
\mathsf{r}^{\prime}
in a single, generalized cut rule called
multicut
.

In this section we study a continuous cut-elimination method that does not rely on multicut rules, following an alternative idea in which the notion of approximation plays an even more central rule, inspired by the topological approaches to infinite trees
[
8
]
. To this end, we assume the reader familiar with basic definitions on domain-theory (see, e.g.,
[
3
]
).

5.1
Approximating coderivations

We introduce
open coderivations
to approximate coderivations.
They form Scott-domains, on top of which we define
continuous cut elimination
.
We also exploit them to
decompose
a finitely expandable and progressing coderivation into a
finite
approximation and a finite sequence of
nwb
s.

Definition 23
.

We define the set of rules
\mathsf{o}\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}\coloneqq\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}\cup\{\mathsf{hyp}\}
, where
\mathsf{hyp}\coloneqq{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 13.23883pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 3.125pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$$}}}\kern 3.125pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{hyp}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to6.25002pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 1.875pt\hbox{$\Gamma$}\kern 1.875pt$}}}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}
for any sequent
\Gamma
.
4
4

4

Previously introduced notions and definitions on coderivations extend to open coderivations in the obvious way, e.g. the global conditions of
Definitions

11
and
17
and the cut-elimination relation
\to_{\mathsf{cut}}
.
We will also refer to
\mathsf{o}\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
as the set of coderivations over
\mathsf{o}\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
, which we call
open coderivations
.
An open coderivation is
normal
if no cut-elimination step can be applied to it, that is, if one premise of each
\mathsf{cut}
is a
\mathsf{hyp}
.
An
open derivation
is a derivation in
\mathsf{o}\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
.
We denote by
\mathsf{o}\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}(\Gamma)
the set of open coderivations with conclusion
\Gamma
.

Definition 24
.

Let

\mathcal{D}
be an open coderivation,

\mathcal{V}\subseteq\{1,2\}^{*}
be a set of mutually incomparable (w.r.t. the prefix order) nodes of
\mathcal{D}
,
and

\{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\nu}\}_{\nu\in\mathcal{V}}
be a set of open coderivations where

\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\nu}
has the same conclusion as the subderivation
\mathcal{D}_{\nu}
of
\mathcal{D}
.
We denote by

\mathcal{D}\{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\nu}/\nu\}_{\nu\in\mathcal{V}}
,
the open coderivation obtained by replacing each
\mathcal{D}_{\nu}
with
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\nu}
.
When
\mathcal{V}
is finite, we will also use the alternative notation
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\nu_{1}}/\nu_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\nu_{n}}/\nu_{n})
.

The
pruning
of
\mathcal{D}
over
\mathcal{V}

is the open coderivation
\lfloor{\mathcal{D}}\rfloor_{\mathcal{V}}=\mathcal{D}\{\mathsf{hyp}/\nu\}_{\nu\in\mathcal{V}}
.
If
\mathcal{D}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
are open coderivations,
we say that
\mathcal{D}
is an
approximation
of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}

(noted
\mathcal{D}\preceq\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
)
iff

\mathcal{D}=\lfloor{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}}\rfloor_{\mathcal{V}}
for some
\mathcal{V}\subseteq\{1,2\}^{*}
.
An approximation is
finite
if it is an open derivation.
We denote by

\mathcal{K}({\mathcal{D}})
(resp.
\mathcal{K}^{f}({\mathcal{D}})
) the set of approximations (resp. finite approximations) of
\mathcal{D}
.

Note that
\mathcal{D}
and
\lfloor{\mathcal{D}}\rfloor_{\mathcal{V}}
(and hence
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
if
\mathcal{D}\preceq\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
) have the same conclusion.
Any open coderivation
\mathcal{D}
is the supremum of its
finite
approximations, i.e.,

\mathcal{D}=\bigsqcup_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K}^{f}({\mathcal{D}})}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
.
Indeed:

Proposition 25
.

For any sequent
\Gamma
,
the poset
\left(\mathsf{o}\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}(\Gamma),\preceq\right)

is a Scott-domain
with least element
the open derivation
\mathsf{hyp}

and
with maximal elements the
coderivations (in
\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
) with conclusion
\Gamma
.
The compact elements are precisely the open derivations in
\mathsf{o}\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}(\Gamma)
.

Cut-elimination steps essentially do not increase the size of open derivations, hence:

Lemma 26
.

\to_{\mathsf{cut}}
over open derivations is strongly normalizing and confluent.

Proof.

For
\mathcal{D}
an open derivation, let
\mathsf{C}(\mathcal{D})
be the number of
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
in
\mathcal{D}
and
\mathsf{H}(\mathcal{D})
be the sum of the sizes of all subderivations
of
\mathcal{D}
whose root is the conclusion of a
\mathsf{cut}
rule.
If
\mathcal{D}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
via:

•

a commutative cut-elimination step, then
\mathsf{C}(\mathcal{D})=\mathsf{C}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime})
,
|\mathcal{D}|=|\mathcal{D}^{\prime}|
and
\mathsf{H}(\mathcal{D})>\mathsf{H}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime})
;

•

a multiplicative cut-elimination (
Figure

3
), then
\mathsf{C}(\mathcal{D})=\mathsf{C}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime})
and
|\mathcal{D}|>|\mathcal{D}^{\prime}|
;

•

an exponential cut-elimination step (
Figure

10
), then
\mathsf{C}(\mathcal{D})>\mathsf{C}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime})
.

Since the lexicographic order over the triples
(\mathsf{C}(\mathcal{D}),|\mathcal{D}|,\mathsf{H}(\mathcal{D}))\in\omega^{3}
is wellfounded, we conclude that there is no infinite sequence
(\mathcal{D}_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
\mathcal{D}_{0}=\mathcal{D}
and
\mathcal{D}_{i}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathcal{D}_{i+1}
.

Finally, since cut-elimination
\to_{\mathsf{cut}}
is strongly normalizing over open derivations
and it is locally confluent by inspection of critical pairs, by Newman’s lemma it is also confluent.
∎

Progressing and finitely expandable coderivations can be approximated in a canonical way. Indeed, by
Lemma

21
we have:

Proposition 27
.

If
\mathcal{D}\in\mathsf{p}\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
is finitely expandable,
then there is a prebar
\mathcal{V}\subseteq\{1,2\}^{*}
of
\mathcal{D}

such that each
v\in\mathcal{V}
is the root of a
nwb
in
\mathcal{D}
.

Definition 28
.

Let
\mathcal{D}\in\mathsf{p}\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
be finitely expandable.
The
decomposition prebar

of
\mathcal{D}
is the
minimal prebar
\mathcal{V}
of
\mathcal{D}
such that, for all
\nu\in\mathcal{V}
,

\mathcal{D}_{\nu}
is a
nwb
. We denote with
\mathsf{border}(\mathcal{D})
such a bar and we set

\mathsf{base}(\mathcal{D})\coloneqq\lfloor{\mathcal{D}}\rfloor_{\mathsf{border}(\mathcal{D})}
.

Note that, by weak König lemma, in the above definition
\mathsf{border}(\mathcal{D})
is finite and
\mathsf{base}(\mathcal{D})
is a finite approximation of
\mathcal{D}
.

5.2
Domain-theoretic approach to continuous cut-elimination

In this subsection we define
maximal and continuous infinitary cut-elimination strategies
(
\mathsf{mc}\text{-}\mathsf{ices}
),
that is, specific rewriting strategies generating infinite reduction chains, whose limits are cut-free open coderivations.
In other words, a
\mathsf{mc}\text{-}\mathsf{ices}
computes a (Scott-)continuous function from open coderivations to cut-free open coderivations. Then, we introduce the
height-by-height

\mathsf{mc}\text{-}\mathsf{ices}
, a notable example of
\mathsf{mc}\text{-}\mathsf{ices}
that will be used for our results, and we show that any two
\mathsf{mc}\text{-}\mathsf{ices}
s compute the same (Scott-)continuous function.

In what follows,
\sigma
denotes a countable sequence of coderivations, and
\sigma(i)
denotes the
(i+1)
-th coderivation in
\sigma
. We denote the length of a sequence
\sigma
by
\mathsf{length}(\sigma)\leq\omega
.

Definition 29
.

An
infinitary cut-elimination strategy
(or
\mathsf{ices}
for short) is a family
\sigma=\{\sigma_{\mathcal{D}}\}_{\mathcal{D}\in\mathsf{o}\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}}
where, for all
\mathcal{D}\in\mathsf{o}\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
,
\sigma_{\mathcal{D}}
is a sequence of open coderivations such that
\sigma_{\mathcal{D}}(0)=\mathcal{D}
and
\sigma_{\mathcal{D}}(i)\to_{\mathsf{cut}}\sigma_{\mathcal{D}}(i+1)
for all
0\leq i<\mathsf{length}(\sigma_{\mathcal{D}})
.
Given an
\mathsf{ices}

\sigma
, we define the function
f_{\sigma}\colon\mathsf{o}\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}(\Gamma)\to\mathsf{o}\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}(\Gamma)
as

f_{\sigma}(\mathcal{D})\coloneqq\bigsqcup_{i=0}^{\mathsf{length}(\sigma_{\mathcal{D}})}\mathsf{cf}{\left(\sigma_{\mathcal{D}}(i)\right)}

where
\mathsf{cf}{\left(\mathcal{D}_{i}\right)}
is the greatest cut-free approximation of
\mathcal{D}_{i}
(w.r.t.
\preceq
).
5
5

5

The function
f_{\sigma}
is well-defined since

\{\mathsf{cf}{\left(\sigma_{\mathcal{D}}(i)\right)}\mid i\geq 0\}
is a direct set in
\left(\mathsf{o}\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}(\Gamma),\preceq\right)
,
therefore its supremum exists by
Proposition

25
.

An
\mathsf{ices}

\sigma
is a
\mathsf{mc}\text{-}\mathsf{ices}
if it is:

•

maximal
:
\sigma_{\mathcal{D}}(\mathsf{length}(\sigma_{\mathcal{D}}))
is normal for any open derivation
\mathcal{D}
(
\mathsf{length}(\sigma_{\mathcal{D}})<\omega
by
Lemma

26
);

•

(Scott)-continuous
:
f_{\sigma}
is Scott-continuous.

Roughly, a maximal
\mathsf{ices}
is an
\mathsf{ices}
that applies cut-elimination steps to open derivations until a normal (possibly cut-free) open derivation is reached.
Together with continuity, this allows us to define
f_{\sigma}(\mathcal{D})
as the supremum of the normal open derivation obtained by applying the cut-elimination steps to the finite approximations of a coderivation
\mathcal{D}
, that is,
f_{\sigma}(\mathcal{D})=\bigsqcup_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K}^{f}({\mathcal{D}})}f_{\sigma}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime})=\bigsqcup_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K}^{f}({\mathcal{D}})}\mathsf{cf}{\left(\sigma_{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(\mathsf{length}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}))\right)}
, where
\mathsf{length}(\sigma_{\mathcal{D}})<\omega
by
Lemma

26
.

The following property states that all
\mathsf{mc}\text{-}\mathsf{ices}
s induce the same continuous function, an easy consequence of
Lemma

26
and continuity.

Proposition 30
.

If
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
are two
\mathsf{mc}\text{-}\mathsf{ices}
s, then
f_{\sigma}=f_{\sigma^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

For any open derivation
\mathcal{D}
, since
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
are maximal, we have that
\sigma_{\mathcal{D}}(\mathsf{length}(\sigma_{\mathcal{D}}))
and
\sigma^{\prime}_{\mathcal{D}}(\mathsf{length}(\sigma^{\prime}_{\mathcal{D}}))
are normal, and so
\sigma_{\mathcal{D}}(\mathsf{length}(\sigma_{\mathcal{D}}))=\sigma^{\prime}_{\mathcal{D}}(\mathsf{length}(\sigma^{\prime}_{\mathcal{D}}))
by
Lemma

26
. Hence:

f_{\sigma}(\mathcal{D})=\mathsf{cf}{\left(\sigma_{\mathcal{D}}(\mathsf{length}(\sigma_{\mathcal{D}}))\right)}=\mathsf{cf}{\left(\sigma^{\prime}_{\mathcal{D}}(\mathsf{length}(\sigma^{\prime}_{\mathcal{D}}))\right)}=f_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\mathcal{D})

Now, let
\mathcal{D}
be an open coderivation.
Since
\mathsf{o}\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
is a Scott-domain (
Proposition

25
), then
\mathcal{D}=\bigsqcup_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K}^{f}({\mathcal{D}})}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
.
We conclude that

f_{\sigma}(\mathcal{D})=\bigsqcup_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K}^{f}({\mathcal{D}})}f_{\sigma}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime})=\bigsqcup_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K}^{f}({\mathcal{D}})}f_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime})=f_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\mathcal{D})

because
f_{\sigma}
and
f_{\sigma^{\prime}}
are continuous.
∎

Therefore, we define a specific
\mathsf{mc}\text{-}\mathsf{ices}
we use in our proofs,
where cut-elimination steps are applied in a deterministic way to the minimal reducible
\mathsf{cut}
-rules.

Definition 31
.

The
height-by-height

\mathsf{ices}

is defined as
\sigma^{\infty}=\{\sigma^{\infty}_{\mathcal{D}}\}_{\mathcal{D}\in\mathsf{o}\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}}

where

\sigma^{\infty}_{\mathcal{D}}(0)=\mathcal{D}
for each
\mathcal{D}\in\mathsf{o}\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
,
and

\sigma^{\infty}_{\mathcal{D}}(i+1)
is the open coderivation obtained by applying a cut-elimination step to the rightmost reducible
\mathsf{cut}
-rule with minimal height in
\sigma^{\infty}_{\mathcal{D}}(i)
.

Proposition 32
.

The
\mathsf{ices}

\sigma^{\infty}
is a
\mathsf{mc}\text{-}\mathsf{ices}
.

Proof.

By
Lemma

26
, any open derivation
\mathcal{D}
normalizes in
n_{\mathcal{D}}\in\mathbb{N}
steps; so, if
\mathcal{D}
is an open derivation,
\mathsf{length}(\sigma^{\infty}_{\mathcal{D}})=n_{\mathcal{D}}
with
\sigma^{\infty}_{\mathcal{D}}(n_{\mathcal{D}})
normal by definition of
\sigma^{\infty}
. Hence,
\sigma^{\infty}
is maximal.
To conclude we have to show that
f_{\sigma^{\infty}}
is continuous.
Since
\sigma^{\infty}_{\mathcal{D}}(i)
is defined by applying a finite number of cut-eliminations steps to
\mathcal{D}
,
then
for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\mathsf{length}(\sigma^{\infty})

there is

\mathcal{D}_{i}\in\mathcal{K}({\mathcal{D}})

such that

\mathsf{cf}{\left(\sigma^{\infty}_{\mathcal{D}}(i)\right)}\preceq f_{\sigma^{\infty}}(\mathcal{D}_{i})
.
Thus
f_{\sigma^{\infty}}(\mathcal{D})\preceq\bigsqcup_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{D})}f_{\sigma^{\infty}}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime})
.
We conclude since
\bigsqcup_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{D})}f_{\sigma^{\infty}}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime})\preceq f_{\sigma^{\infty}}(\mathcal{D})
because
\sigma^{\infty}
is monotone by construction.
∎

Example 33
.

For any finite approximation
\mathcal{D}
of the (non-weakly progressing, non-finitely expandable) open coderivation
\mathcal{D}_{\!\lightning}
, we have
f_{\sigma^{\infty}}(\mathcal{D})=\mathsf{hyp}
, so
f_{\sigma^{\infty}}(\mathcal{D}_{\!\lightning})=\mathsf{hyp}
by continuity.

5.3
Productivity of Continuous Cut-elimination

We conclude this section by proving continuous cut-elimination theorem, the main contribution of this paper,
establishing a productivity result and showing that continuous cut-elimination preserves all global conditions.
For this purpose, we introduce the notion of chain of
\mathsf{cut}
-rules, which allows us to keep track of the dynamic of cut-elimination steps during infinitary rewriting.
Note that the definition of
\mathsf{cut}
-chain is the analogue of the
multi-cut reduction sequences
from
[
5
]
.

Definition 34

(Chains)
.

Let
\sigma=\{\sigma_{\mathcal{D}}\}_{\mathcal{D}\in\mathsf{o}\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}}
be an
\mathsf{ices}
.
We write

\mathsf{r}_{i}\mapsto_{\sigma}\mathsf{r}_{i+1}

if

\mathsf{r}_{i+1}
is a
\mathsf{cut}
-rule in
\sigma_{\mathcal{D}}(i+1)

produced by applying a cut-elimination step to
the
\mathsf{cut}
-rule
\mathsf{r}_{i}
in
\sigma_{\mathcal{D}}(i)
. A
\mathsf{cut}
-chain

in
\sigma_{\mathcal{D}}
is a maximal sequence
(\mathsf{r}_{i})_{i<\alpha}
of
\mathsf{cut}
rules
with
\alpha\leq\mathsf{length}(\sigma_{\mathcal{D}})
,
such that

\mathsf{r}_{i}
a rule in
\sigma_{\mathcal{D}}(i)
,
and
either
\mathsf{r}_{i}=\mathsf{r}_{i+1}
or
\mathsf{r}_{i}\mapsto_{\sigma}\mathsf{r}_{i+1}
. It is

stable
if (it is infinite and) there is a
k\geq 0
such that
\mathsf{r}_{i}=\mathsf{r}_{k}
for all
i\geq k
.

Notation 35
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a coderivation.
The
starting point
of a thread
\tau
in
\mathcal{D}
is its first element.
A
progressing point
of a
\oc
-thread
\oc\rho
in
\mathcal{D}
is a sequent which is conclusion of a
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
-rule with principal
\oc
-formula the one in
\oc\rho
.

A
\mathsf{cut}
-rule is
reducible
if a cut-elimination step can be applied to it, that is, if one of its premises is not the conclusion of a
\mathsf{cut}
-rule.

We denote by
\sigma^{\infty}_{\tau}(i)
the thread of
\sigma^{\infty}_{\mathcal{D}}(i)
obtained by applying
\sigma^{\infty}
to
\tau
(notice that, if it exists, it is unique).

Lemma 36
.

Let
\mathcal{D}\in\mathsf{p}\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
. If
\mathcal{D}
is not cut-free, then it contains a reducible
\mathsf{cut}
-rule.

Proof.

By definition, if no
\mathsf{cut}
-rule in
\mathcal{D}
is reducible, then both premises of each
\mathsf{cut}
-rule in
\mathcal{D}
are conclusion of
\mathsf{cut}
-rules.
Therefore, if
\mathcal{D}
is not cut-free, then any rule above a
\mathsf{cut}
-rule is a
\mathsf{cut}
-rule.
This implies the existence of an infinite branch containing only
\mathsf{cut}
-rules, contradicting progressivity of
\mathcal{D}
.
∎

Lemma 37
.

Let
\mathcal{D}\in\mathsf{p}\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
and
(\mathsf{r}_{j})_{j\geq 0}
an infinite
\mathsf{cut}
-chain in
\sigma^{\infty}_{\mathcal{D}}
with
\mathsf{r}_{0}
in
\mathcal{D}
.
Then:

1.

If
(\mathsf{r}_{j})_{j\geq 0}
is not stable, then there is no
i_{0}\geq 0
such that, for every
i\geq i_{0}
, either
\mathsf{r}_{i+1}=\mathsf{r}_{i}
or
\mathsf{r}_{i}\mapsto_{\sigma}\mathsf{r}_{i+1}
is a commuting step or a
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\mbox{-vs-}\wn\mathsf{b}
step.

2.

(\mathsf{r}_{j})_{j\geq 0}
is not stable;

3.

there is some
i_{0}\geq 0
and a
\oc
-thread
\tau
of
\sigma_{\mathcal{D}}(i_{0})
such that, for every
i\geq i_{0}
:

(a)

the
\oc
-formula that is active in
\mathsf{r}_{i}
is the starting point of
\sigma^{\infty}_{\tau}(i)
,

(b)

if
\mathsf{r}_{i}\mapsto_{\sigma}\mathsf{r}_{i+1}
then
\mathsf{r}_{i}
is a
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\mbox{-vs-}\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
, a
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\mbox{-vs-}\wn\mathsf{b}
or a commuting step,

(c)

there are infinitely many
\mathsf{r}_{i}\mapsto_{\sigma}\mathsf{r}_{i+1}
such that
\mathsf{r}_{i}
is a
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\mbox{-vs-}\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
step.

Proof.

Concerning
Item

1
, suppose
(\mathsf{r}_{j})_{j\geq 0}
is not stable, and there is some
i_{0}\geq 0
such that, for every
i\geq i_{0}
, either
\mathsf{r}_{i+1}=\mathsf{r}_{i}
or
\mathsf{r}_{i}\mapsto_{\sigma}\mathsf{r}_{i+1}
is a commuting step or a
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\mbox{-vs-}\wn\mathsf{b}
step. We first show that the set
I
of
i\geq i_{0}
such that
\mathsf{r}_{i}\mapsto_{\sigma}\mathsf{r}_{i+1}
is a
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\mbox{-vs-}\wn\mathsf{b}
step is finite. To this end, we notice that the cut-elimination step
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\mbox{-vs-}\wn\mathsf{b}
erases a
\wn\mathsf{b}
rule
\mathsf{r}
and creates a finite sequence of
\wn\mathsf{b}
rules in the same branch of
\mathsf{r}
; no other cut-elimination step creates
\wn\mathsf{b}
rules. This means that, if
I
were infinite, either a branch containing infinite
\wn\mathsf{b}
rules (and only finitely many
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
rules) exists already in
\mathcal{D}
, or
\mathcal{D}
contains a branch with infinitely many
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
rules whose principal
\oc
-formula will be eventually active for a cut of the form
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\mbox{-vs-}\wn\mathsf{b}
. Both cases are impossible by progressivity of
\mathcal{D}
. Therefore, there is
i_{1}\geq i_{0}
such that, for every
i\geq i_{1}
, either
\mathsf{r}_{i+1}=\mathsf{r}_{i}
or
\mathsf{r}_{i}\mapsto_{\sigma}\mathsf{r}_{i+1}
is a commuting step. Moreover, since
(\mathsf{r}_{j})_{j\geq 0}
is not stable there must be infinite commuting steps. We apply a similar reasoning and appeal to progressivity of
\mathcal{D}
.

To prove
Item

2
, it suffices to show that there is
i\geq 0
such that
\mathsf{r}=\mathsf{r}_{0}\neq\mathsf{r}_{i}
. Suppose towards contradiction that
\mathsf{r}_{i}=\mathsf{r}
for every
i\geq 0
. By
Lemma

36
, for every
i\geq 0
there is a
\mathsf{r}^{\prime}_{i}\neq\mathsf{r}_{i}
that is reduced at the
i
-th step of cut-elimination. Let
h_{j}
and
k_{i}
be the heights of
\mathsf{r}_{i}
and
\mathsf{r}^{\prime}_{i}
in
\sigma^{\infty}_{\mathcal{D}}(i)
respectively.
By definition of
\sigma^{\infty}
, it must be that either
k_{i}<h_{j}
or
k_{i}=h_{i}
and
\mathsf{r}^{\prime}_{i}
is at the right of
\mathsf{r}
(remember that
\sigma^{\infty}
applies a cut-elimination step to the rightmost reducible cut with minimal height). Notice that there cannot be infinitely many
i
with
k_{i}<h_{i}
. Indeed, this would induce a cut-elimination sequence
\sigma^{\infty}_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}}
with infinite length on a finite approximation
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{D}
, contradicting
Lemma

26
. This means that there is
i_{0}\geq 0
such that
k_{i}=h_{i}=h_{i_{0}}
for every
i\geq i_{0}
, and
\mathsf{r}^{\prime}_{i}
is at the right of
\mathsf{r}_{i}
. We observe that there are only finitely many (reducible) cuts at height
h_{i_{0}}
in any
\sigma^{\infty}_{\mathcal{D}}(i)
, and moreover any cut-elimination step applied to a cut with height
h_{i_{0}}
produces at most one cut rule with the same height. We conclude that infinitely many
\mathsf{r}^{\prime}_{i}
belong to the same cut-chain, which is therefore not stable. Furthermore, the only cut reduced by this cut-chain are
\invamp\mbox{-vs-}\otimes
and
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\mbox{-vs-}\wn\mathsf{b}
, since these are the only cut-elimination steps that reduce a cut producing a new one with the same height.
However, since
\invamp\mbox{-vs-}\otimes
-steps shrink the size of the cut-formulas, there must be infinitely many
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\mbox{-vs-}\wn\mathsf{b}
-steps in the cut-chain. This contradicts
Item

1
.

Let us finally show
Item

3
. Note that since
(\mathsf{r}_{j})_{j\geq 0}
is infinite, no
\mathsf{ax}\mbox{-vs-}\mathsf{cut}
or
\wn\mathsf{w}\mbox{-vs-}\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
cut-elimination step is ever applied to
\mathsf{r}_{j}
, otherwise the chain would be finite.
Also, there are finitely many
i\in\mathbb{N}
such that the active formulas of
\mathsf{r}_{j}
are
\invamp
- or
\mathop{\otimes}
-formulas, because each
\mathop{\otimes}\mbox{-vs-}\invamp
cut-elimination step reduces the size of the
\mathsf{cut}
-formula. Notice also that there are finitely many
\wn\mathsf{b}\mbox{-vs-}\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
steps
\mathsf{r}_{i}\mapsto\mathsf{r}_{i+1}
such that
\mathsf{r}_{i+1}
is the topmost cut produced because, again, this cut-elimination step decreases the size of the formula. Therefore, there exists
i_{0}\geq 0
and a
\oc
-thread
\tau
of
\sigma_{\mathcal{D}}(i_{0})
such that, for every
i\geq i_{0}
the
\oc
-formula that is active in
\mathsf{r}_{i}
is the starting point of
\sigma^{\infty}_{\tau}(i)
. This shows
Item

3a
. Moreover,
Item

3b
follows by the fact that any
\mathsf{r}_{i}
with
i\geq i_{0}
has exponential active formulas and the fact that
\mathsf{r}_{i}\mapsto\mathsf{r}_{i+1}
is neither
\mathsf{ax}\mbox{-vs-}\mathsf{cut}
not
\wn\mathsf{w}\mbox{-vs-}\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
. By
Item

1
and
Item

2
, this implies
Item

3c
.

∎

We introduce some terminology to trace
\mathsf{cut}
-rules during cut-elimination.
The names yard, clewline and buntline are borrowed from the sailing jargon.
The yard is a horizontal spar on which a square sail is attached.
6
6

6

See
https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yard_(sailing)
.

The clewlines and buntlines are lines attached respectively to the corner and to the foot of a square sail, which is furled up on the yard.
7
7

7

See
https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clewlines_and_buntlines
.

The intuition is that, during cut-elimination, a yard eventually moves upwards together with the end-points of the buntlines, which are attached to the foot of the sail.
Meanwhile the clewlines are pulled down-ward.
We then use the existence of such a clewline for each
\mathsf{cut}
-chain to ensure the existence of a progressing thread in each infinite branch created by eliminating the
\mathsf{cut}
-rules of an infinite
\mathsf{cut}
-chain.

Definition 38
.

A
yard
is an infinite
\mathsf{cut}
-chain
(\mathsf{r}_{i})_{i<\alpha}
such that if

\mathsf{r}_{i+1}\neq\mathsf{r}_{i}
, then
\mathsf{r}_{i}
is the
\leq_{\mathcal{T}}
-minimal right-most
\mathsf{cut}
-rule produced by the cut-elimination step applied to
\mathsf{r}_{i}
.

A thread
\tau
is a
buntline
of a yard
(\mathsf{r}_{i})_{i\geq 0}

if there is a
i_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that the starting point of
\sigma^{\infty}_{\tau}(i)
is the active formula of
\mathsf{r}_{i}
for all
i\geq i_{0}
.

A
\oc
-thread
\oc\rho
is a
clewline
of a yard
(\mathsf{r}_{i})_{i\geq 0}

if there is a
i_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that:

•

the starting point of
\sigma^{\infty}_{\oc\rho}(i)
is not the active formula of a
\mathsf{cut}
-rule in
\sigma^{\infty}_{\mathcal{D}}(i)
for any
i\geq 0
;

•

the conclusion of
\mathsf{r}_{i}
contains a formula in
\sigma^{\infty}_{\oc\rho}(i)
for any
i\geq i_{0}
;

•

For any
k>0
there is a
i_{k}\geq i_{0}
such that
\sigma^{\infty}_{\oc\rho}(i_{k})
has at least
k
points right below
\mathsf{r}_{i_{k}}
in
\sigma^{\infty}_{\mathcal{D}}(i_{k})
.

Lemma 39

(Furl up the sail!)
.

Let
\mathcal{D}\in\mathsf{p}\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
.
If
(\mathsf{r}_{j})_{j\geq 0}
is a yard in
\sigma^{\infty}_{\mathcal{D}}
, then it has a (unique) clewline
\oc\rho
.

Proof.

By
Lemma

37
.
3a
there is a buntline
\tau
of the yard
(\mathsf{r}_{j})_{j\geq 0}
. Moreover, by definition and
Lemma

37
.
3b
and
Lemma

37
.
3c
,
there is a
i_{0}\geq 0
such that:

•

\mathsf{r}_{i_{0}}
is of the form
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\mbox{-vs-}\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
,

•

for every
i\geq i_{0}
, if
\mathsf{r}_{i}\mapsto_{\sigma}\mathsf{r}_{i+1}
then
\mathsf{r}_{i}
is a
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\mbox{-vs-}\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
, a
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\mbox{-vs-}\wn\mathsf{b}
or a commuting step.

Moreover, it must be that one of the
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
rules right above
\mathsf{r}_{i_{0}}
has principal
\oc
-formula active for
\mathsf{r}_{i_{0}}
, and the other
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
rule has principal
\oc
-formula that appears both in the premise and in the conclusion of
\mathsf{r}_{i_{0}}
. Let
\oc\rho
be the
\oc
-thread containing the latter
\oc
-formula (it is unique by
Remark

13
). It is easy to check that, for all
i\geq i_{0}
:

1.

the starting point of
\sigma^{\infty}_{\oc\rho}(i)
is not the active formula of a
\mathsf{cut}
-rule in
\sigma^{\infty}_{\mathcal{D}}(i)
,

2.

the conclusion of
\mathsf{r}_{i}
contains a formula in
\sigma^{\infty}_{\oc\rho}(i)
.

This follows by the fact that every cut in a yard has minimal height and the fact that every
\mathsf{r}_{i}\mapsto_{\sigma}\mathsf{r}_{i+1}
with
i\geq i_{0}
is a
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\mbox{-vs-}\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
, a
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\mbox{-vs-}\wn\mathsf{b}
or a commuting step. To conclude that
\oc\rho
is a clewline we observe that by
Lemma

37
.
3c
there are infinitely many
j\geq 0
such that
\mathsf{r}_{j}\mapsto_{\sigma}\mathsf{r}_{j+1}
is a
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\mbox{-vs-}\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
step. By
Items

1
and
2
the principal
\oc
-formula of one of the two
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
rules right above
\mathsf{r}_{j}
has a progressing point of
\oc\rho
. Then, the sub-coderivation of
\sigma^{\infty}_{\mathcal{D}}(j)
containing
\sigma^{\infty}_{\oc\rho}(j)
and the sub-coderivation of
\sigma^{\infty}_{\mathcal{D}}(j+1)
containing
\sigma^{\infty}_{\oc\rho}(j+1)
are of the following form,
where we mark with

☞

the progressing points of
\oc\rho
in focus.

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

This shows that there are infinitely many
j
such that
\sigma^{\infty}_{\oc\rho}(j+1)
has strictly more progressing points right below
\mathsf{r}_{j+1}
then
\sigma^{\infty}_{\oc\rho}(j)
has below
\mathsf{r}_{j}
. Moreover, we observe that the number of progressing points of
\oc\rho
that are below cuts in the yard cannot decrease. Therefore,
\oc\rho
is a clewline.

∎

We now have all technical tools requires to prove the so-called
productivity
result for
f_{\sigma^{\infty}}
,
that is,
that if
\mathcal{D}
is a progressing coderivation,
then

f_{\sigma^{\infty}}(\mathcal{D})
is a (well-defined)
\mathsf{hyp}
-free and
\mathsf{cut}
-free coderivation.
In addition, we show that the same reasoning we use to ensure productivity
also ensures that the progressing condition is preserved by
f_{\sigma^{\infty}}
.

Theorem 40
.

If
\mathcal{D}\in\mathsf{p}\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
, then so is
f_{\sigma^{\infty}}(\mathcal{D})
.

Proof.

We have to prove that if
\mathcal{D}\in\mathsf{p}\mathsf{PLL}^{\!\infty}
, then
f_{\sigma^{\infty}}(\mathcal{D})
is a well-defined
\mathsf{cut}
-free coderivation,
that is,

f_{\sigma^{\infty}}(\mathcal{D})
contains no
\mathsf{hyp}
-rule and any infinite branch in
f_{\sigma^{\infty}}(\mathcal{D})
contains a progressing
\oc
-thread.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a branch of
f_{\sigma^{\infty}}(\mathcal{D})
.
If
\mathcal{B}
is also in
\sigma^{\infty}_{\mathcal{D}}(k)
for a
k\in\mathbb{N}

(therefore in all
\sigma^{\infty}_{\mathcal{D}}(j)
with
j\geq k
),
then it must be
\mathsf{hyp}
-free by definition of the cut-elimination steps
and,
in case it is infinite, it has a progressing
\oc
-thread by
Proposition

22
.

Otherwise there is no
j\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\mathcal{B}
is a branch of
\sigma^{\infty}_{\mathcal{D}}(j)
.
In this case,

•

either
there is an infinite
\mathsf{cut}
-chain
(\mathsf{r}_{j})_{j\geq 0}
in
\sigma^{\infty}_{\mathcal{D}}
and a
j_{0}\in\mathbb{N}

such that the branch
\mathcal{B}_{j}
of
\sigma^{\infty}_{\mathcal{D}}(j)
containing the conclusion of
\mathsf{r}_{j}
is an initial segment of
\mathcal{B}
for all
j\geq j_{0}
.
Note that this
\mathsf{cut}
-chain is a yard by construction, and that the sequence
(\mathcal{B}_{j})_{j\geq 0}
is well-ordered with supremum
\mathcal{B}
by definition of
\sigma^{\infty}
.

In this case, by
Lemma

39
there is clewline for
(\mathsf{r}_{j})_{j\geq 0}
and there is a
j_{0}
such that for all
j\geq j_{0}
if
\mathsf{r}_{j}\neq\mathsf{r}_{j+1}
, then
\mathcal{B}_{j+1}
strictly contains
\mathcal{B}_{j}
and the number of progressing points of the clewline below
\mathsf{r}_{j+1}
increases.
We conclude that
\oc\rho
is the progressing
\oc
-thread of
\mathcal{B}
, and that
\mathcal{B}
is
\mathsf{hyp}
-free by construction because it is infinite;

•

or
we can define a sequence
(\mathsf{r}_{j})_{j\geq 0}
of
\mathsf{cut}
-rules such that

\mathsf{r}_{j}
is the bottom-most
\mathsf{cut}
-rule in the initial segment
\mathcal{B}_{j}
of
\mathcal{B}
in
\sigma^{\infty}_{\mathcal{D}}(j)
.
As in the previous case we have that the sequence
(\mathcal{B}_{j})_{j\geq 0}
is well-ordered with supremum
\mathcal{B}
by definition of
\sigma^{\infty}
.

In this case
(\mathsf{r}_{j})_{j\geq 0}
is the union of finite
\mathsf{cut}
-chains, that is,
we have that
(\mathsf{r}_{j})_{j\geq 0}=\prod_{k_{i}}(\mathsf{r}_{k_{i}},\ldots,\mathsf{r}_{(k_{i+1}-1)})
for some
k_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
with
0=k_{0}
and
k_{i}<k_{i+1}
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
.
Thus we have that
\mathsf{r}_{j+1}
is either obtained by applying a cut-elimination step to
\mathsf{r}_{j}
, or that
j+1=k_{i}
if a cut-elimination step applied to
\mathsf{r}_{j}
introduces no new
\mathsf{cut}
-rules.
By progressiveness of
\mathcal{D}
, we know that the infinite branch of
\mathcal{D}
containing the conclusion of all
\mathsf{r}_{k_{i}}
contains a progressing thread
\tau
. Then, since any cut-chain in
(\mathsf{r}_{j})_{j\geq 0}
is finite, there must be some
j_{0}\geq 0
such that the starting point of
\sigma^{\infty}_{\tau}(j)
is not the active formula of
\mathsf{r}_{j}
for any
j\geq j_{0}
. This means that
\mathcal{B}
is progressing and
\mathsf{hyp}
-free.

∎

Remark 41
.

In our setting, yards play the same role of multicuts (see, e.g.,
[
5
,
14
,
17
,
1
,
2
]
).
To be more precise, the same role of the bottom-most
\mathsf{cut}
-rule in a pile of
\mathsf{cut}
-rules is represented by the multicut.
Note that, at the pure syntactical level, if we consider a multicut as a rule and we define multicut-elimination steps, then the number of the premises of such a rule may increase indefinitively during cut-elimination.
In fact, the derivation
\mathcal{D}_{\!\lightning}
in
Figure

7
would reduce, via multicut-elimination, to a (progressing) derivation made of a multicut with infinite premises conclusion of
\mathsf{ax}
-rules.

{{}{{}{}}\vbox{\hbox{\kern 2.66388pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 9.36938pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 13.09723pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$$}}}\kern 13.09723pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{${A}^{\bot},A$}\kern 0.0pt$}}}}}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 10.00002pt}\hbox{\kern 12.03326pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 9.09721pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\vdots$}}}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma,A$}\kern 0.0pt$}}}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 9.36938pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{cut}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to66.42218pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{cut}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to90.53604pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}\kern 24.11386pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 45.54018pt\hbox{\hbox{$\Gamma,A$}}\kern 60.28465pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\qquad\to_{\mathsf{cut}}^{*}\qquad{{}{}{}{}\vbox{\hbox{\kern 17.7139pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 9.36938pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 12.26392pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$$}}}\kern 12.26392pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox
